## Assistant

The image shows a **single-threaded event loop combined with a worker thread pool**:

1. Clients submit requests to an event queue.
2. One event-loop thread dispatches requests.
3. Non-blocking operations stay on the asynchronous path.
4. Blocking I/O is delegated to a worker thread, which returns its result afterward.

Its overall layout is useful, but **two parts need adapting for our C# server**:

| Reference image | Our ASP.NET Core server |
|---|---|
| One central, single-threaded event loop | Async connection handlers whose continuations execute on .NET thread-pool threads |
| Database I/O blocks a worker thread | EF Core async database calls release the executing thread while awaiting I/O |

Microsoft recommends awaiting asynchronous APIs for I/O-bound work; wrapping blocking I/O in `Task.Run` still occupies a worker thread. [Microsoft guidance](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/asynchronous-programming/async-scenarios)

I suggest using the reference’s **architecture-style layout** for a clearer concurrency overview:

- **Left:** Clients A, B, and C, each with TCP connections.
- **Inside the server:** async receive handlers → message routing → Room 1 and Room 2 queues → sequential room processors.
- **Below the processors:** per-client outbound queues → async send loops → clients.
- **Above the processing area:** a shared **“.NET Thread Pool — executes ready continuations and game logic”** box.
- **Right:** SQL Server connected through **EF Core async I/O**.

Add a note: **“Pending network/database I/O does not reserve a worker thread. Each room processes one action at a time.”**

This would explain the concurrency approach more directly than our current detailed swimlane diagram. **No changes applied yet.**

    +
    +## Additional overview page
    +
    +At the user's request, added a separate `Concurrency Overview` page using the
    +supplied event-loop/thread-pool image as a layout reference. The existing
    +`Server Concurrency` page was explicitly protected and remains byte-for-byte
    +unchanged, as do all other pre-existing pages.
    +
    +The new overview adapts the reference to .NET rather than introducing a single
    +global event-loop thread or worker-blocking database I/O:
    +
    +- WinForms Clients A and B route to Room 1; Client C routes to Room 2.
    +- Per-connection asynchronous receive handlers frame and validate messages.
    +- A router forwards actions to per-room queues with sequential consumers.
    +- A shared .NET thread pool executes ready continuations and game logic;
    +  dashed execution links illustrate this logical relationship, not dedicated
    +  workers reserved for particular rooms or clients.
    +- Private and broadcast events pass through recipient filtering and bounded
    +  per-client outbound queues, then ordered asynchronous send loops.
    +- EF Core asynchronous persistence accesses SQL Server over TDS with TLS.
    +- Pending network/database I/O does not reserve a worker thread. Disconnects,
    +  failures, and full outbound queues trigger cleanup and room notifications.
    +
    +The file now contains six pages. XML parsing, unique IDs, graph references,
    +relative edge geometry, and attribute escaping checks passed. No rendered
    +preview was available; final appearance should be checked in draw.io.

## User

is this non-blocking I/O

## Assistant

Yes. The design uses **asynchronous, non-blocking I/O**: while `AcceptTcpClientAsync()`, `ReadAsync()`, `WriteAsync()`, or an asynchronous database operation is pending, no thread is held waiting for it.

The **thread pool executes ready code**—such as parsing messages and processing room actions. It does not reserve a thread for each pending I/O operation.

For the brief, **“Asynchronous non-blocking I/O”** is accurate.

## User

give me finalized biref

## Assistant

**Concurrency Model: Event-Driven + Thread Pool + Asynchronous Non-Blocking I/O**

Network events trigger asynchronous handlers. The .NET thread pool executes ready continuations and game logic. Pending network and database I/O does not block a waiting thread. Each room processes actions sequentially, while different rooms run concurrently.

## User

clarify what is an event ?

## Assistant

An **event** is something that happens and causes the server to respond.

In your game, examples include:

- **Connection accepted:** start handling a new client.
- **Data received:** assemble a message and route the player’s action.
- **Timer expires:** end a turn or begin voting.
- **Database operation completes:** continue processing with the result.
- **Client disconnects:** remove their connection and update the room.

A network event and a game action are different: **“data received”** means bytes arrived; after decoding those bytes, the server may discover a **DRAW**, **GUESS**, or **VOTE** action.

Here, “event-driven” describes how work becomes ready. It does **not** require using C#’s `event` keyword.

Pending network and database I/O does not occupy a waiting thread. Each room processes actions sequentially, while different rooms run concurrently.

    # Diagram editing instructions
    
    ## Project and assignment
    
    - Act as a C4 system design collaborator with C# expertise.
    - Project: multiplayer Draw & Guess Game with Normal and Imposter modes.
    - The user's assignment is system architecture: overall architecture and
      multithreading/multiple-connection diagrams. C4 Context, C4 Container, and the

      slides 2–3. The supplied assignment screenshot further divides team duties.
    - At least two GUI clients, a server-side database, a team-developed network
      stack, and concurrent connections are required. Detailed ports, framing,
      packet/message definitions, game flows, GUI wireframes, and database schema
      belong to other assigned work unless the user expands the scope.
    
    ## Editing workflow
    
    - Brainstorm and present proposed architectural changes before applying them.
      Once the user authorizes a concrete change, apply it without asking again.
    - Record design decisions and changes in the corresponding log:
      `c4-context-design-log.md`, `c4-container-design-log.md`, or
      `server-concurrency-design-log.md`. Preserve these separate files.

      page is a separate activity/swimlane view; leave it untouched when editing
      Concurrency Overview unless the user explicitly requests otherwise.
    - Do not automatically change displayed titles and page names together.
    
    ## Established architecture
    
    - Client: C# / Windows Forms.
    - Server: C# / ASP.NET Core; Minimal API endpoints and a custom TCP listener
      share one process and one C4 container.
    - Gameplay uses a team-developed application protocol over TCP, not SignalR.
    - Registration, sign-in, and session acquisition use HTTPS / JSON.
    - Database: Microsoft SQL Server. Server data access uses EF Core with the
      SQL Server provider; the diagram's database connection is TDS over TLS.
    - Google Identity is an external software system. Google sign-in uses the
      system browser and OpenID Connect / HTTPS; detailed authentication flow
      remains an implementation decision to coordinate with its owner.
    - Accounts are required to play. Host is a Player with room creation, mode
      selection, and game-start controls.
    - The system chooses keywords from its default pool. The host does not supply
      the keyword and may be assigned the imposter role.
    - In Imposter mode, n−1 players know the keyword; the imposter tries to infer it
      and blend in. The server controls turns, votes, reveal, and results. Do not
      invent unresolved Normal-mode rules, imposter drawing rules, tie handling,
      host transfer, or reconnect policies.
    - The server owns authoritative state and limits secret keyword/role delivery
      to eligible recipients. Active room state initially lives in server memory.
    
    ## Concurrency model
    
    - Event-driven asynchronous non-blocking I/O with .NET thread-pool execution.
      Events include incoming connections, received data, timer expiry, and
      disconnections; this does not require C#'s `event` keyword.
    - Use asynchronous receive/send tasks per connection, not dedicated threads
      per client. Pending network/database I/O does not reserve a waiting thread.
    - The thread pool executes ready continuations and game logic. Do not depict
      a single global event-loop thread or routine blocking database calls handed
      to workers as the chosen architecture.
    - Route actions to one sequential processing queue per room. Different rooms
      may progress concurrently; each room's state changes are serialized.
    - Use bounded outbound queues and one ordered asynchronous send loop per
      client. Slow clients must not block room processing; disconnect/failure/full
      queue handling triggers cleanup and queued membership removal.
    - Preserve room ordering when an asynchronous database result affects state.
    - The concurrency diagram is a supporting architecture view, not an extra C4
      abstraction level. The activity view and architecture overview may coexist.
    
    ## Layout and validation
    
    - Keep generous space between actors and software systems/containers, and
      place legends well away from the main drawing.
    - Use explicit line breaks and sufficient shape dimensions for descriptions.
      Use the correctly cased draw.io style `whiteSpace=wrap` on wrapped nodes.
    - Give edge labels explicit widths where needed. Keep short arrow labels
      horizontal and non-wrapping; place them in open space above horizontal
      segments. Avoid narrow labels that stack words or individual characters.
    - Minimize connector bends and crossings. Preserve readable parallel routes;
      use a shared execution annotation instead of many crossing scheduler arrows.
    - Keep the model brief at the top left of Concurrency Overview in its own
      reserved space, explaining events, the thread pool, non-blocking I/O, and
      sequential room processing. Shift waypoints consistently when moving nodes.
    - Validate XML parsing, IDs and graph references per page, and relative edge
      geometry. Compare unrelated pages and protected legend contents with their
      originals. Inspect a rendered result when possible; report honestly when
      rendering is unavailable. XML validity alone does not prove visual quality.

    The new overview adapts the reference to .NET rather than introducing a single
    global event-loop thread or worker-blocking database I/O:
    
    - WinForms Clients A and B route to Room 1; Client C routes to Room 2.
    - Per-connection asynchronous receive handlers frame and validate messages.
    - A router forwards actions to per-room queues with sequential consumers.
    - A shared .NET thread pool executes ready continuations and game logic;
      dashed execution links illustrate this logical relationship, not dedicated
      workers reserved for particular rooms or clients.
    - Private and broadcast events pass through recipient filtering and bounded
      per-client outbound queues, then ordered asynchronous send loops.
    - EF Core asynchronous persistence accesses SQL Server over TDS with TLS.
    - Pending network/database I/O does not reserve a worker thread. Disconnects,
      failures, and full outbound queues trigger cleanup and room notifications.
